      "raw_content": "# Israel Food Guide: Must-Try Dishes, Prices, and Tips\n\nBest local dishes, street food, restaurant prices, dietary options, and food safety tips for Israel.\n\nBy Hello Travel Team\n\n![Israel Food Guide: Must-Try Dishes, Prices, and Tips](https://images.unsplash.com/photo-1542743408-5fdf746fd87d)\n\nIn this article\n\n## TL;DR: What to Eat in Israel and How Much It Costs\n\nIsrael food guide basics: the country is one of the easiest places to eat well on almost any budget, with **street food meals often costing ₪25-60 ($7-16)** and casual restaurant mains usually landing around **₪60-140 ($16-38)** in 2026. A quick lunch from a falafel stand might cost **₪25-40 ($7-11)**, while a casual café meal usually comes in around **₪45-80 ($12-22)**.\n\nFor a better feel for what you’ll spend, use this comparison:\n\n| Meal type | Typical price in 2026 (ILS) | Typical price in 2026 (USD) |\n| --- | --- | --- |\n| Falafel or sabich pita | ₪18-45 | $5-12 |\n| Hummus plate | ₪25-45 | $7-12 |\n| Street shawarma | ₪35-60 | $10-16 |\n| Casual restaurant main | ₪60-140 | $16-38 |\n| Mid-range dinner with drink | ₪140-250 | $38-68 |\n| Upscale tasting menu | ₪300+ | $82+ |\n\nTaxes and service can change the final bill, and in sit-down places a meal can rise quickly once you add drinks, starters, and dessert. If you’re traveling with friends, Hello’s expense splitting and budget tracking tools make it easy to divide shared mezze dinners, taxi rides to restaurants, and market snack runs without manual math.\n\n## Best Areas to Eat in Israel: Where Foodies Should Go\n\nThe most rewarding food neighborhoods in Israel are usually the ones with the best mix of markets, bakeries, and old-school counters, not just fancy dining rooms. In **[Israel](/israel)**, the strongest food scenes are in **Tel Aviv**, **Jerusalem**, **Haifa**, and the smaller market towns where lunch spots stay busy all day.\n\nIn Tel Aviv, look for café breakfasts, modern Israeli plates, and late-night street food around **Carmel Market**, **Florentin**, and the city center. Jerusalem is ideal for hummus, pastries, and Middle Eastern classics, especially around the **Mahane Yehuda Market** area. Haifa is excellent for mixed Arab-Jewish food traditions, and it’s one of the best places to try fresh seafood, baked goods, and family-run lunch spots.\n\nOne useful rule: if a place is packed with locals around lunch, the food is usually fresher and better priced. If you’re mapping out a food crawl, Hello’s trip planning and eSIM connectivity help you navigate between neighborhoods, check live reviews, and keep your itinerary moving without hunting for Wi-Fi.\n\n## Dietary Options, Halal, Vegetarian, and Vegan Dining in Israel\n\nIsrael is one of the easiest destinations in the region for **vegetarian and vegan dining**, and halal options are widely available in Arab neighborhoods, mixed cities, and many grill restaurants. If you eat plant-based, you can often build whole days around hummus, falafel, salads, roasted vegetables, tahini, and bakery items without feeling limited.\n\nVegetarian staples include shakshuka, sabich, salads, stuffed pastries, and dairy breakfasts, while vegan travelers should look for dishes marked **parve** or ask whether breads, sauces, and pastries contain dairy or eggs. Halal dining is common in Jerusalem, Nazareth, Acre, Jaffa, and many other cities, especially at shawarma, kebab, and family-run Middle Eastern restaurants. Kosher restaurants are also widespread, but keep in mind that many close on **Shabbat**, so Friday afternoon and Saturday can mean reduced hours or no service.\n\nA practical ordering tip: if you want to avoid confusion, ask staff whether a dish is **halal**, **vegan**, or **parve**, because menus are not always fully translated. Fresh salads, dairy-heavy breakfast dishes, and hot grilled foods are common, and tap water is widely used in urban areas, though many travelers still prefer bottled water for convenience.\n\nWhen ordering street food, choose vendors with active lines, covered ingredients, and food that is cooked to order. For restaurant tipping, **10-12% is a normal range** at sit-down places if service is not already included, while rounded-up cash tips are common for quick counter service. Taste local specialties, hear the stories behind the stalls, and experience the city like a true insider.  \n👉 [Book the Carmel Market Food Tour](https://www.beinharimtours.com/tel-aviv-food-tour-carmel-market/)\n\n## 🥘 **25 top-rated Tel Aviv restaurants**\n\n### Port Said: the Tel Aviv icon\n\n📍[Har Sinai St 5](https://goo.gl/maps/miCx61uw874sYVGc7)  \n💲 from $14 USD\n\nPort Said is an iconic restaurant in Tel Aviv, known for its innovative fusion of Middle Eastern and Mediterranean cuisines, served in a hip, lively atmosphere. The venue combines retro and modern decor near the Great Synagogue, with vinyl records section and eclectic music playing.\n\nThe menu, designed by famed chef Eyal Shani, features creative dishes like the baked cauliflower and the beef tartare. Not only that Port Said is one of the top Tel Aviv restaurants but this is also a great spot for cocktails or pre-drinks.\n\n### Anastasia Cafe: best vegan\n\n![tel aviv restaurants](https://www.psimonmyway.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/04/Anastasia-Tel-Aviv-Restaurants.jpg \"25 essential Tel Aviv Restaurants: where and what to eat in Israel's food capital 5\")\n\n“Open Table” also comes with a salad, hummus on the side, and homemade bread!\n\n📍[Frishman Street 54](https://goo.gl/maps/7QiF1JEaCbgkTFKr6)  \n💲 from $12 USD\n\nAnastasia Tel Aviv is actually a vegan restaurant and is considered one of the best in Tel Aviv but if you’re not a vegan, it will work, too.\n\nThis restaurant makes the best vegan cheeses in the world and their desserts are always worth a try even if you’re too full! Anastasia is always full of people, so expect to wait in line on weekends.\n\nThis restaurant in Tel Aviv is more popular as a breakfast place as it is very central. You can call to try to make a reservation but in my experience, they only do this every Friday (Shabbat).\n\nThey serve food really fast and the staff is very efficient. My favorite breakfast plate here is the ‘Open Table’ which is a variation of house spreads – sunflower aioli cream, spicy cilantro chutney, and labneh which is a thick yogurt-based cheese.\n\n### A by Yuval Ben Neriah: meticulous Japanese\n\n📍[Derech Menachem Begin 121](https://goo.gl/maps/GeP28JzfdPmMPq216)  \n💲from $35 USD  \n🏆 Middle East’s 50 Best Restaurants\n\nLed by the esteemed Chef Yuval Ben Neriah, the kitchen creates masterful dishes showcasing a symphony of flavors and textures, drawing influences from across the Mediterranean and Middle East.\n\nHighlights from the menu include fresh seafood, succulent meats, and vibrant vegetables prepared with artistic flair. A Restaurant also has the most extensive [sake](https://www.britannica.com/topic/sake) menu in Tel Aviv that can be ordered in different *shuki* sizes (from $12 – $248 USD).\n\n### Nola: best American breakfast\n\n📍[Dizengoff St 197](https://goo.gl/maps/WaTswRUBwTF5WxMPA)   \n💲from $13 USD\n\nI’ve never been to New Orleans but my Israeli friends always say Nola has that vibe. They serve fresh bread daily and have a generous portion of American breakfasts.\n\nI came here often for coffee in the afternoon as they also have great spaces for digital nomads/online workers. You will see a lot of these people hanging out (and working) at Nola.\n\nBoth their indoor and outdoor seating are great – choose wherever you want to sit as long as it’s not full. And oh, try their cookie selection, too!\n\nYou can order bread from Nola to take home. They bake their bread every day! The staff is not too friendly so you might want to skip this if you’re looking for great service. I only go here when I crave American breakfast.\n\n### Animar: best Mediterranean\n\n📍 [Retsif Herbert Samuel St 94](https://goo.gl/maps/bm8DEzU3ndYphoKv7)  \n💲 from $25 USD  \n🏆 Middle East’s 50 Best Restaurants\n\nAnimar is a mediterranean-centric restaurant with a space that presents a stylish backdrop for the menu’s centerpiece – fresh, locally sourced seafood, simply but masterfully prepared.\n\nTheir menu change seasonally, showcasing the best of Israel’s marine and agricultural bounty. The raw bar is particularly impressive, offering an array of fresh oysters, tartares, and ceviches.\n\nThe kitchen also prepares delectable meat and vegetable dishes, if you’re not a fan of seafood. Make sure to ask for the best Israeli wines to pair with the dishes – their servers are highly knowledgeable about pairings.\n\n> **Note:**Animar is located inside the [Dan Hotel](https://booking.tp.st/JTmO3otB) in Tel Aviv.\n\n### Cafe Yom Tov: best outdoor brunch\n\n![tel aviv restaurants](https://www.psimonmyway.com/wp-content/uploads/2019/04/Yom-Tov-Cafe-Tel-Aviv-Restaurants.jpg \"25 essential Tel Aviv Restaurants: where and what to eat in Israel's food capital 9\")\n\nYom Tov Cafe: Our favorite Friday spot where we can have brunch and go day drinking!\n\n📍[Yom Tov St 30](https://g.page/yomtovcafe?share)   \n💲 from $12 USD\n\nI don’t know about you but sometimes, I just want to sit in a nice environment for breakfast and just be quiet. Yom Tov (good day in English), offers that kind of vibe — it has full outdoor seating in a street where people normally pass by.\n\nThe passersby won’t bother you that much, I swear. Take it as an opportunity to do people-watching in Tel Aviv. Its close proximity to the Carmel Market is the reason the ingredients are fresh!\n\nThis restaurant is not only for breakfast as, during mid-day, but it also turns into a trendy restaurant where you can have your lunch and dinner, too.\n\nI went here a lot and the best tip I can give you is to never come here for Friday brunch – you’ll wait hours to get a table. On weekdays, come before 10:00 am as it tends to be full after this hour.\n\n### Thai House\n\n📍 [Bograshov St 8](https://goo.gl/maps/x6Fz2CrzUU8wwkZx5)  \n💲 from $53 USD\n\nA slice of Bangkok in Tel Aviv, Thai House offers the most authentic Thai cuisine in the city. This isn’t your average Pad Thai joint; we’re talking Thai food so authentic, you’ll question if you’re still in Israel.\n\nThe “Green Curry with Eggplant and Tofu” and “Tom Yam Soup” are as good as it gets outside of Bangkok. Complement your meal with the “Thai Mojito,” a refreshing cocktail with an infusion of lemongrass and galangal.\n\nBe sure to make a reservation, this place gets packed!\n\n### George & John: best restaurant in Israel\n\n📍 [Auerbach St 6](https://goo.gl/maps/CzzGSz18tzR17KFRA)  \n💲 from $85 USD  \n🏆 Middle East’s 50 Best Restaurants\n\nGeorge and John is an upscale restaurant situated in the historic [Drisco Hotel](https://booking.tp.st/wJ7ysmw7) in Tel Aviv’s trendy Neve Tzedek district. Housed in a restored 19th-century building, the restaurant exudes old-world charm complemented by chic modern touches.\n\nThe menu, crafted by Chef Tomer Tal, explores the diversity of Israeli cuisine, with a focus on locally sourced, seasonal ingredients. Dishes are beautifully presented, featuring bold flavors and innovative combinations.\n\nStandout dishes include the fresh fish of the day and the slow-cooked lamb. The wine list is equally impressive, offering a wide range of Israeli and international wines. George & John is a stylish dining destination with a nod to heritage.\n\n### Dok: best Jewish cuisine\n\n📍 [Shlomo Ibn Gabirol St 27](https://goo.gl/maps/wiSraKwqaR4hVXQr7)  \n💲 from $35 USD\n\nDok is a boutique farm-to-table restaurant nestled in the heart of Tel Aviv. This restaurant takes pride in sourcing Israeli produce, ensuring the freshest ingredients for its inventive, rotating menu.\n\nFYI, the menu changes almost daily so it’s possible you’ll visit Dok more than once during your [trip to Tel Aviv!](https://www.psimonmyway.com/solo-travel-to-tel-aviv-israel/)\n\nThis intimate restaurant radiates a relaxed and warm ambiance, featuring rustic elements that reflect its agricultural ethos. There are 2 small tables and a bar that can seat 8-people with an open kitchen where you can see all the action.\n\n### HaBasta: best local market spot\n\n📍 [HaShomer St 4](https://goo.gl/maps/MAjuykXP2zgoGyY8A)  \n💲 from $12 USD  \n🏆 Middle East’s 50 Best Restaurants\n\nHaBasta is a vibrant restaurant located next to Tel Aviv’s bustling Carmel Market. Its name, meaning “The Stall,” is fitting, as it brings the market’s fresh ingredients straight to the table.\n\nThe menu changes daily to reflect the freshest finds, often incorporating playful twists on traditional dishes. The ambiance is casual and lively, reflecting the market’s energy.\n\nLocals and tourists alike relish the diverse menu, which could include anything from freshly caught fish, to hearty meat dishes, to innovative vegetarian options. With its intimate seating, friendly staff, and inventive cuisine, HaBasta is a dynamic slice of Tel Aviv’s culinary scene.\n\n### M25: best barbecue\n\n📍[Simtat HaCarmel 30](https://goo.gl/maps/xj4wSGED2xP4DJ1WA)  \n💲 from $12 USD\n\nM25 is a vibrant, meat-centric restaurant located in the heart of Tel Aviv’s Carmel Market. Named for its location within the meat market, M25 serves a menu of locally-sourced, expertly-prepared meats and they sell uncooked ones, too!\n\nThe restaurant’s industrial design, featuring exposed brick and butcher-block tables, enhances its market ambiance. Highlights of the menu include tender steaks, succulent kebabs, and juicy burgers, all from the grill!\n\nComplemented by a selection of fresh salads and innovative sides, the dishes celebrate the flavors of the Middle East.\n\n### Opa: plant-based paradise\n\n📍 [Ha-Khalutzim St 8](https://goo.gl/maps/FRPkMdQAfiBFXRwx5)  \n💲 from $30 USD\n\nTucked in the trendy Levinsky Market district, Opa is a plant-based foodie’s paradise. Here, vegetables are the stars of the show.\n\nChef Shirel Berger’s vegan creations, like the “Kohlrabi Ravioli with Fermented Tomato Sauce,” are sublime and will change your perception of vegan food.\n\nNo detail is overlooked, right down to the cocktail list. “The Garden,” a vodka-based cocktail with celery, green apple, and elderflower, is a must-try. Also try the “Opa Cocktail,” a beautifully balanced mix of gin, St. Germain, lemon, and grapefruit.\n\n### Benedict: 24/7 breakfast\n\n📍 [Rothschild Blvd 29](https://goo.gl/maps/8Mx8bctw2h59uWqC7)   \n💲 from $13 USD\n\nI don’t remember intentionally going to Benedict for breakfast but it was always a routine after going out of the Breakfast Club, a popular nightclub in Tel Aviv.\n\nI’ve only been to the Rothschild branch as it’s a popular avenue but I heard the other branches are good, too. Benedict is open 24/7 – it will feed your breakfast craving any time of the day!\n\nBenedict is not somewhere you’ll go intentionally. I just go to Benedict because it’s always just there when I go out of the club. It’s always nice to know that you have somewhere to go after a heavy nightlife in Tel Aviv and Benedict is it!\n\n### Milgo & Milbar: best upscale seafood\n\n📍[Rothschild Blvd 142](https://goo.gl/maps/XhvDyqkynKQmHTjYA)  \n💲from $65 USD  \n🏆 Middle East’s 50 Best Restaurants\n\nMilgo & Milbar is an upscale restaurant with an exciting fusion of Middle Eastern and Mediterranean cuisines. The ambiance is sophisticated yet relaxed, with sleek modern interiors.\n\nHighlights from the menu include a variety of fresh seafood dishes, succulent meats, and unique vegetarian options. The “Sea Fish Fillet with Cauliflower Cream” is an absolute crowd-pleaser. For vegetarians, the “Baked Eggplant with Tahini” is a delightful treat.\n\nAlongside the food, Milgo & Milbar offers an impressive wine list featuring a selection of local and imported wines. And if you’re leaning towards a cocktail, their “Spiced Pear Martini” featuring pear-infused vodka, lime, and honey is as delicious as it sounds.\n\n### Cafe Xoho: best all-day breakfast\n\n📍[J. L. Gordon St 17](https://goo.gl/maps/Kmxv6o1cvpbg74m57)   \n💲from $10 USD\n\nThis is almost everyone’s #1 favorite, including the vegans! Cafe Xoho’s interior has great interiors that are really pleasing to see in the morning (or all day!)\n\nThe owner, Xoli Ormut Durbin was born to Canadian parents in Hong Kong, lived in Australia then moved to Tel Aviv.\n\nCan you imagine how much fusion and diversity this person has and that upbringing reflects on Cafe Xoho’s menu. They have an all-day breakfast and don’t you ever NOT try their cookies – they’re really good!\n\n### **OCD Restaurant**: sustainable restaurant award\n\n📍[Tirtsa St 17](https://goo.gl/maps/Y5RMRpu6FqH8mXQb6)  \n💲from $58 USD  \n🏆 Middle East’s 50 Best Restaurants\n\nYou know how you sometimes wish for a fully immersive dining experience that goes beyond just food on a plate? Well, OCD Restaurant in Tel Aviv is where it’s at.\n\nWith an open kitchen and communal seating, it’s all about dining as a shared experience. Every detail, from the ingredient selection to the presentation, is meticulously thought out, hence the name.\n\nOCD’s 16-course tasting menu changes every night, adapting to what’s fresh and in season. The real standout might be the “Red Tuna Sashimi with Watermelon Radish and Pomegranate.”\n\nTheir curated cocktail pairings elevate the whole experience. Do try the “Gin & Dill,” a cocktail that pays homage to local ingredients.\n\n### Abu Hassan: best hummus\n\n📍[Shivtei Israel St 14](https://goo.gl/maps/9FNcbtrrXbMegWdq9)   \n💲from $8 USD\n\nAbu Hassan is a legendary establishment famed for serving arguably the best hummus in the city. A simple yet authentic setting, with a busy and vibrant atmosphere, this Jaffa-based restaurant should be on top of your Tel Aviv restaurants list!\n\nObviously, you have to order the delectable and creamy hummus, traditionally garnished with fresh parsley, and olive oil. The hummus comes with fluffy pitas and crunchy pickles and can be shared by two (or not).\n\n### Pronto: best Italian\n\n📍 [Herzl St 4](https://goo.gl/maps/8T963ddSpvq9Kiyr6)  \n💲 from $53 USD\n\nWhen the craving for Italian food hits in Tel Aviv, Pronto is where you should be headed. This hotspot feels like a chic Italian trattoria with standout dishes like homemade gnocchi and risotto.\n\nThe “Home-made Gnocchi with Ox Cheek Ragout” is comfort food at its finest. Pair it with the classic “Negroni” cocktail, and you’ll feel like you’ve been transported to a Roman holiday.\n\n### Pastel: modern brasserie\n\n📍 [Sderot Sha’ul HaMelech 27](https://goo.gl/maps/YvcG9iEKnGrxJKRs7)  \n💲 from $45 USD\n\nHoused in the Tel Aviv Museum of Art, Pastel serves art on plates with a chic brasserie feel. It offers a fusion menu with standout dishes like lamb sirloin and red tuna sashimi.\n\n \n\nThis modern brasserie showcases the best of Israeli produce with European flair. The “Lamb Sirloin with Crispy Artichoke” is a stunner. Complement it with the “Pastel Margarita,” made with tequila, triple sec, lime, and cilantro!\n\nTheir wine list is a carefully curated collection of local and imported selections. Finish your meal with their signature “Chocolate Story” dessert, and a glass of dessert wine.\n\n### **Hotel Montefiore Restaurant**: upscale brunch\n\n📍 [Montefiore St 36](https://goo.gl/maps/YpQhfF9NMC6JDZCr6)  \n💲 from $25 USD\n\nHotel Montefiore Restaurant is a high-end dining destination known for its unique blend of Vietnamese-French cuisine with a local twist. Housed in a meticulously restored 1920s building, on the street with the same name, the restaurant exudes an old-world charm balanced by contemporary design elements.\n\n \n\nStandouts include the duck confit spring roll and pan-seared sea bream. The establishment also boasts a well-curated wine list, showcasing both Israeli and international selections.\n\nHotel Montefiore is bests for special occasions and Friday brunches. Just make sure to reserve since Friday is a busy day for Tel Aviv restaurants!\n\n### Shila: best raw seafood\n\n📍 [Ben Yehuda St 182](https://goo.gl/maps/ic9KUiQpxNWhvPvVA)  \n💲 from $78 USD\n\nShila isn’t just another stop in the bustling Tel Aviv dining scene, it’s an institution. Known for its lively ambiance, you’ll find a mix of locals and travelers here, all drawn in by the promise of a night filled with good food and fun.\n\n \n\nAlthough it’s known for a variety of dishes, Shila truly shines with its seafood offerings. The “Seafood Linguini” is a flavorful symphony of fresh catch tossed in a rich, herby sauce. But the real standout is the “Shrimps in Garlic Butter.”\n\nWash it all down with their “Lavender Mule,” a fragrant twist on the classic cocktail, featuring vodka, fresh lime, ginger beer, and a dash of lavender.\n\n📍 Yishkon St 29  \n💲 from $13 USD\n\n### Sholomo and Doron: another best hummus\n\n📍 [Yishkon St 29](https://goo.gl/maps/TNDamEWFdUxE4G5Z8)  \n💲 from $13 USD\n\nShlomo and Doron is another enduring hummus institution nestled in the heart of Tel Aviv’s historic Kerem Hateimanim neighbourhood. Owned by the same family for over 80 years, the restaurant is steeped in tradition, with the original recipes passed over generations.\n\n \n\nIt serves a selection of rich, creamy hummus dishes topped with a variety of ingredients, like warm chickpeas, fragrant tahini, or spicy *shakshuka*. Their culinary creativity extends to reinventions like the “Yemenite meat soup hummus”.\n\n### Taizu: upscale Asian street food\n\n📍[Derech Menachem Begin 23](https://goo.gl/maps/u6PiTRXVX5zz2B9i6)  \n💲 from $70 USD\n\nTaizu will whisk your taste buds off on a journey through Southeast Asia, without leaving Tel Aviv. With a menu inspired by street food, each dish, like the “Vietnamese Rice Rolls,” is bursting with flavor. Be sure to try the dim sums!\n\n \n\nPair your food with the “Yuzu Sour,” a cocktail made with whisky, yuzu, ginger, and bitters for a complete culinary experience. Martini lovers, start the dinner with their “Ginger Lychee Martini”!\n\n## ⁉️ FAQ: Tel Aviv food and restaurants\n\nTel Aviv is renowned for its diverse cuisine but is primarily known for its hummus, falafel, and shawarma. It’s also gaining recognition for its vegan and farm-to-table offerings.\n\nIsraelis typically eat dinner later in the evening, often starting around **8 or 9 pm.**\n\nIsrael is famous for several dishes but hummus, falafel, shawarma, and shakshuka often top the list. For desserts, baklava and halva are popular choices.\n\nThe cost of a meal in Tel Aviv varies greatly depending on the restaurant, but expect to pay around 50-100 ILS ($14-$27 USD) for a meal at an average restaurant.\n\nYes, tipping is common in Israel. It’s customary to leave a tip of about **10-15% at restaurants**, though it’s always discretionary.\n\n## 🇮🇱 Tel Aviv travel recommendations\n\n* Find your way through local markets, unique eateries, and traditional restaurants, exploring the city’s rich culinary history and diverse flavors with a [food tour in Tel Aviv](https://viator.tp.st/8gARWlSd).\n* Discover Tel Aviv’s dynamic street art scene through a [walking graffiti tour](https://viator.tp.st/XAAwYF7B). Uncover hidden gems in alleyways, explore colorful murals in hip neighborhoods, and learn about the city’s culture through the eyes of its artists.\n* Explore the winding [alleyways of Jaffa](https://viator.tp.st/VtB6xx7D), one of the world’s oldest ports, rich in history and culture. Visit ancient landmarks, marvel at the stunning architecture, and experience the vibrant atmosphere of bustling markets.\n* Experience [Tel Aviv’s 24/7 nightlife](https://viator.tp.st/IJRCCZDo) in the Rothschild area. Dive into the city’s thriving music scene, sip cocktails in stylish bars, and dance until dawn in underground clubs.\n\n* **Carmel Market (Shuk HaCarmel):** This bustling outdoor market is a feast for the senses, offering a variety of fresh produce, local spices, baked goods, clothing, and more. A great place to experience local life and taste traditional Israeli food.\n* **Tel Aviv Museum of Art:** Home to a broad spectrum of contemporary and classic art, the museum hosts works by leading Israeli and international artists. The stunning architecture is a masterpiece itself.\n* **Jaffa Port and Old City:** Steeped in history, Jaffa offers a mix of ancient ruins, art galleries, and trendy boutiques. The picturesque port area offers stunning views and excellent dining options.\n* **Neve Tzedek:** Tel Aviv’s first neighborhood, Neve Tzedek’s charming streets are filled with beautifully restored Bauhaus-style buildings, boutiques, and cafes. It’s a hub of culture and architecture.\n* **Tel Aviv Beaches:** Stretching along the city’s western edge, the beaches offer a perfect spot for sunbathing, surfing, volleyball, or simply enjoying the lively beachside promenade.\n\n* **Shila – Sharon Cohen’s Kitchen & Bar:** A culinary hotspot in Tel Aviv, Shila serves modern Israeli cuisine in a lively, bustling environment. Known for its top-quality meats and seafood, the menu offers a variety of dishes that reflect Israel’s diverse food culture.\n* **Port Said:** A local favorite for Middle Eastern cuisine, Port Said offers a creative menu with an Israeli twist. The vibrant setting, excellent food, and inventive cocktails, combined with music from vinyl records, create a unique and enjoyable dining experience.\n* **Ouzeria:** Offering a Mediterranean-inspired menu, Ouzeria serves dishes that showcase the richness of Israeli cuisine. Its stylish decor, creative and flavorful food, and extensive wine list make it a great place for a culinary adventure.\n* **Radio EPGB:** This underground bar offers a unique mix of music, art, and alcohol. Known for its eclectic crowd and electric energy, it’s an ideal place for meeting new people and enjoying Tel Aviv’s vibrant nightlife.\n* **Sputnik:** A trendy bar in the heart of Tel Aviv’s nightlife district, Sputnik offers a large outdoor space, cozy corners, and creative cocktails. It draws a friendly, diverse crowd, making it a great spot for socializing.\n\n* **[The Norman Tel Aviv](https://booking.tp.st/gOW51ZIL):** A luxury boutique hotel in the heart of Tel Aviv, The Norman blends the elegance of the 1920s with modern comfort. It features beautifully designed rooms, a rooftop pool with city views, a wellness area, and top-class dining options.\n* **[Brown TLV Urban Hotel](https://booking.tp.st/jX95EBIV):** Centrally located in Tel Aviv, this boutique hotel offers comfortable, stylish rooms with a vintage touch. Enjoy amenities like a rooftop lounge, complimentary bicycles for city exploration, and a cozy bar. The personalized service and convenient location make it an excellent choice for a mid-range accommodation option.\n* **[Florentine Hostel](https://hostelworld.tp.st/JVdYNZOy):** This modern, clean hostel offers a range of private and dormitory rooms in the hip Florentin neighborhood. Its communal kitchen, cozy lounge area, and friendly staff provide an excellent opportunity to meet fellow travelers. Its location offers easy access to restaurants, shops, and nightlife.\n* Israel is currently experiencing a **housing shortage for locals**because of platforms like Airbnb or Vrbo. The local community would appreciate it if you book local-run hotels and accommodations.\n\n* Tel Aviv is a walking city so there’s no need to worry about transportation. You can walk anywhere, take a bus, or rent a bike. Just make sure to book an [airport taxi transfer](https://viator.tp.st/ld64ub30) as the airport is far away from the center.\n\n* Don’t travel to Tel Aviv without insurance. [Ekta Traveling](https://ektatraveling.tp.st/5v4PRxxy) is the recommended insurance (for travelers ages 2 months to 100 years old).\n\n![](https://www.psimonmyway.com/wp-content/wphb-cache/gravatar/96f/96fdb2dead90c4838e914f6ba7a2f39ax90.jpg)\n\n##### Trisha Velarmino\n\nTrisha is one of those people who left their comfortable life to travel the world and learn about life. Her style is to stay in one place she likes for 3 months (or more) to know what it feels like to eat, cook, speak, and sleep in another culture that isn’t hers. She'd like to believe she's not traditionally traveling but she just chooses to be somewhere else all the time. In no particular order, her favorite cities in the world are Barcelona, Buenos Aires, Hong Kong, and Mexico City.\n\nLoading comments...\n\n* [📣 Start Here](https://www.psimonmyway.com/)\n* [🗺️ Explore](https://www.psimonmyway.com)\n  + [💻 Digital Nomad](https://www.psimonmyway.com/digital-nomad/)\n  + [✈️ Solo Female Travel](https://www.psimonmyway.com/solo-travel/)\n  + [🇵🇭 Filipino Passport Travel](https://www.psimonmyway.com/tag/travel-guides-for-filipinos/)\n* [🥂 Brand Partnerships](https://www.psimonmyway.com/work-with-me/)\n* [🙋🏼‍♀️ 1-1 coaching](https://www.psimonmyway.com/shop/travel/1-1-coaching/)\n* [📩 Newsletters](https://www.psimonmyway.com/newsletters/)\n* [✈️ Shop](#)\n  + [Travel Maps](https://www.psimonmyway.com/shop-category/travel-maps/)\n* [$0](https://www.psimonmyway.com/cart/ \"Cart\") \n  + No products in the cart.\n\n    [Return to shop](https://www.psimonmyway.com/shop/)\n* [Login / Register](https://www.psimonmyway.com/my-account/ \"Login\")\n\n[Secret Link](https://www.psimonmyway.com/aefa8f05b86ddca4)\n\n ",

      "url": "https://www.israel.com/places/jaffa/restaurants",
      "title": "Where to eat in Jaffa: 13 places, kashrut stated | Israel.com",

      "raw_content": "Israel votes on Tuesday, 27 October\n\n![Old Jaffa on its headland at dusk, with the Tel Aviv towers behind it](https://images.unsplash.com/photo-1547483029-77784da27709?ixid=M3wxMDE5Mzc0fDB8MXxzZWFyY2h8MXx8SmFmZmElMjBvbGQlMjBwb3J0JTIwSXNyYWVsfGVufDF8MHx8fDE3ODYwODk1NzF8MA&ixlib=rb-4.1.0&w=1600&h=667&fit=crop&crop=entropy&auto=format&q=75)\n\nJaffa · Where to eat\n\n# Where to eat in Jaffa\n\nJaffa eats Palestinian at the base and fish on top, and of the thirteen places below exactly one publishes a kashrut certificate.\n\n[Shai Pal](https://unsplash.com/@shaipal?utm_source=israel_com&utm_medium=referral) / [Unsplash](https://unsplash.com/photos/aerial-view-photography-of-city-beside-body-of-water-yBJuiaMHlsk?utm_source=israel_com&utm_medium=referral)\n\nPicks here that publish a kashrut certificate\n\nOne\n\nFattal's page for The Jaffa Hotel calls it a kosher hotel and Golda's a kosher dairy restaurant. The other twelve say nothing either way.\n\nIsraeli VAT, already in the menu price\n\n18%\n\nThe rate since 2025. Nothing else is added at the end of an Israeli bill, because the tip is not on it.\n\nTips became wages on\n\n1 Jan 2019\n\nThe National Labour Court ruled in March 2018 that restaurant tips are salary, carrying income tax, pension and national insurance.\n\nAbu Hassan's first shop on Ha-Dolphin Street\n\n1972\n\nAli Karavan had been selling hummus from a cart since 1959, on Asif's account of it. The shop still closes when the food runs out.\n\nJaffa is a small eating district with an unusually wide price range inside it. The whole of it fits in about a square kilometre: Kedumim Square and the lanes below it, the fishing harbour, the flea market four streets inland, and Jerusalem Boulevard running away from the clock tower into the ordinary town. Within that square kilometre a fried fish lunch at the port and a dinner with the Mediterranean in the window are a hundred metres and a very large sum apart, and both are worth having.\n\nThe cooking is mostly Palestinian and mostly built on the same two habits. First, the table fills before anything is ordered: salads, pickles, tahini, hot bread, and in the Levantine houses that spread belongs to the price of the main course rather than sitting beside it on the bill. Second, the sea is the protein. Jaffa is a working fishing harbour with a few dozen boats and the fish restaurants are the oldest commercial fact about the place. Around that base sit the newer things, a Greek room on the square, an Asian kitchen in a church building, a bar with a band in the flea market, and a knafeh counter that put ice cream in the middle of it.\n\nThe one thing to settle before booking anything is kashrut, because Jaffa is the least kosher place to eat in Israel and does not advertise the fact. Twelve of the thirteen entries below publish nothing on the subject in either direction, two of them serve shellfish that answers the question for them, and one, a hotel dining room, states plainly that it is certified. Where an operator publishes its hours, its owners or its kashrut, that is taken from its own page and linked at the foot of this guide. Where nothing is published, the entry says so rather than guessing.\n\n13 places to eat\n\n## The places to eat worth the trip in Jaffa\n\nOur pick and why, then the listing's own details, dated.\n\n01\n\n### Kabakeh\n\nGalilean Palestinian cooking above Kedumim Square\n\nKabakeh is the Jaffa offshoot of Ezba, the Galilean Arab restaurant at Rameh that its own booking page says has been running for close to three decades, and it cooks from the same tradition: bulgur kibbeh, dumplings in yoghurt, and dishes built on wild greens and on whatever the season is doing. It occupies an old stone house on the edge of Kedumim Square, which is the most photographed spot in Jaffa and very rarely the site of a kitchen this serious. The published week is short and catches people out in both directions: Wednesday to Saturday only, from five in the afternoon midweek and from midday on Friday and Saturday. Of everything on this page it is the one most worth arranging a trip around.\n\nBook through OnTopo. It does not open Sunday, Monday or Tuesday, so a short trip has to be built around it rather than the other way round.\n\n89% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun-Tue | Closed |\n| Wed | 17:00-23:00 |\n| Thu | 17:00-23:30 |\n| Fri | 12:00-23:30 |\n| Sat | 12:00-22:00 |\n\n02\n\n### The Old Man and the Sea\n\nThe table that fills before you order\n\nThe format is the whole point. Sit down and the table disappears under small plates, salads, pickles, tahini, hot pitta from a tabun oven and fresh lemonade, all of it before a main course has been discussed, and then the fish arrives in quantities built for sharing. Its own site dates the restaurant from 1999 and puts fried prawns, calamari and grilled fish at the centre of the menu, which also settles its kashrut without anyone needing to ask. It is loud, fast, on the water and designed for families and groups rather than for a quiet dinner, and it is the single most efficient way to feed six people well in Jaffa. Go hungry and order less than instinct suggests.\n\nBook ahead for Friday and Saturday and for any group. Reservations by telephone and WhatsApp through its own site.\n\n79% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun-Sat | 11:00-00:00 |\n\n![A restored sandstone house behind a high stone wall and dark iron gate on a narrow Jaffa lane, with bougainvillea flowering over the wall to the right](/_next/image?url=%2Fsite-photos%2Fsite-jaffa-ajami.webp&w=3840&q=75)\n\n[שלמה רודד](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:116106_affluent_houses_after_restoration_-_jaffa_PikiWiki_Israel.jpg \"שלמה רודד\") / CC BY 2.5 · [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:116106_affluent_houses_after_restoration_-_jaffa_PikiWiki_Israel.jpg)\n\n03\n\n### Saint George\n\nA long grill in restored stone\n\nSaint George works the fish houses' arrangement with the grill at the centre instead: kofta, lamb chops and kebab, preceded by a spread of mezze that arrives unasked and is generous enough to be the meal. It sits in a restored stone house on Louis Pasteur Street, the short lane below Kedumim Square that now holds several of the neighbourhood's better known Arab kitchens side by side, and the kitchen is open to the room. It is more comfortable than the harbour counters and noticeably cheaper than the tables with a sea view a minute's walk uphill, which makes it the sensible middle of this page. It publishes nothing about kashrut in either direction.\n\nBookings by telephone or through OnTopo. Worth holding a table on a Friday or Saturday evening, when the whole lane fills at once.\n\n84% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun-Sat | 11:30-23:30 |\n\n04\n\n### Kalamata\n\nDinner with the sea in the window\n\nKalamata is the Greek room on Kedumim Square, named after the town in the Peloponnese and its olives, and its booking page sells the view before it sells the kitchen. That is fair enough: it is a two-level stone building between the fishing harbour and the old town, the good tables are the ones on the water side, and the Greek music and the anise are part of the offer. The cooking is Greek with eastern Mediterranean edges, and there is a cheaper fixed menu at lunchtime on weekdays. The same page lists 11:00 to 23:00 on Friday and Saturday, which makes it one of the easier good tables to get on a weekend evening.\n\nOnTopo through its own site, and ask for the sea side when you book. The difference between the two halves of this restaurant is the reason to come.\n\n83% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun | 11:00-17:00 |\n| Mon-Sat | 11:00-23:00 |\n\n![Mown lawn and the stepped stone seating of the hilltop park at Jaffa, with palm trees to the right and the Tel Aviv skyline along the coast beyond](/_next/image?url=%2Fsite-photos%2Fsite-jaffa-hapisga-garden.webp&w=3840&q=75)\n\n[Anagoria](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:20191027_083943a_Abrasha_Park_Gan_Hapisga_Yaffa_Tel_Aviv_anagoria.jpg \"Anagoria\") / CC BY 3.0 · [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:20191027_083943a_Abrasha_Park_Gan_Hapisga_Yaffa_Tel_Aviv_anagoria.jpg)\n\n05\n\n### Cafe Puaa\n\nBreakfast in the flea market's front room\n\nPuaa was in the flea market long before the lanes filled with places to eat, and it more or less invented the format the others copied: a room furnished entirely from the stalls outside, with a price tag on the chair, the lamp and the plate the shakshuka arrives on. The cooking is home-style Mediterranean and strongest in the morning and at lunch, and the masala chai has a following of its own. It is not a secret, it is usually full and it is not trying to be either of those things. Come early on a weekday and sit outside on Rabbi Yohanan Street where the market is still doing its actual job.\n\n88% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun-Wed | 09:00-22:30 |\n| Thu | 09:00-23:00 |\n| Fri | 09:00-22:30 |\n| Sat | 10:00-22:30 |\n\n06\n\n### Raisa\n\nSaturday brunch when the stalls are shut\n\nRaisa is an owner-run bistro in the market whose proprietor writes on the restaurant's own site that her door is open to everybody and that the fish patties and lamb cigars are the best in Jaffa, which is a claim a small restaurant is entitled to make about itself. It publishes a full seven-day week including Saturday from ten in the morning, with a separate brunch menu, which makes it one of the better answers to the question of what is open on a day when the market around it is not. The plates are tapas-sized, there is a proper cocktail list, and the room is small enough that a Friday or Saturday evening without a booking is a gamble. Order several things rather than one.\n\nOnTopo through its own site, or by telephone. The Saturday brunch is the service most worth holding a table for.\n\n83% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun-Wed | 12:00-23:00 |\n| Thu | 12:00-00:00 |\n| Fri | 09:00-00:00 |\n| Sat | 10:00-23:00 |\n\n![The stone facade of the Ilana Goor Museum in Old Jaffa, its painted name below a roof terrace lined with sculptures and rows of pottery jars, bougainvillea flowering against the wall](/_next/image?url=%2Fsite-photos%2Fsite-jaffa-ilana-goor-museum.webp&w=3840&q=75)\n\n[Deror\\_avi](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Goor_P1040977.JPG \"Deror_avi\") / CC BY-SA 3.0 · [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Goor_P1040977.JPG)\n\n07\n\n### Captain Hook\n\nOysters on a flea market corner\n\nCaptain Hook stands on the corner of Amiad and Rabbi Hanina in the loudest part of the flea market and does fish and seafood with a mezze front end. What separates it from the fry counters down at the harbour is the raw bar: it is an oyster bar as much as a fish restaurant, which puts it beyond any question of kashrut and beyond the reach of anyone who wants a certificate. Tables spill onto the street, there is usually music within earshot, and it works far better as a long evening than as a quick meal. This is a place to sit at for three hours, not to fit in before something else.\n\nWorth booking for a weekend evening. Street tables go first and are the ones people come for.\n\n86% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun | Closed |\n| Mon-Sat | 11:30-23:00 |\n\n08\n\n### Faruk BaShuk\n\nFriday afternoon with a band playing\n\nFaruk is on Nakhman Street in the middle of the flea market and is as much a bar with a kitchen as a restaurant. Its own site publishes exactly what it is for: happy hour Sunday to Thursday between five and eight, live music on Tuesday evenings at eight and again on Friday afternoons at four, and a Friday brunch before that. The Friday slot is the useful one, because four in the afternoon is the hour the market stops selling furniture and starts selling drinks, and Faruk is where that changeover is most audible. The food is Mediterranean and Italian and honestly secondary to the room.\n\nBook through its own site for the Friday afternoon music. Weekday evenings are usually a walk-in.\n\n82% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun-Wed | 10:00-23:00 |\n| Thu-Sat | 10:00-00:30 |\n\n![The Ottoman stone clock tower with its dark spire standing in Jaffa's empty square in early morning light, with palm trees and arcaded stone buildings behind it](/_next/image?url=%2Fsite-photos%2Fsite-jaffa-jaffa-clock-tower.webp&w=3840&q=75)\n\n[Sasha1506](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:20211010_073054_Clock_Square.jpg \"Sasha1506\") / CC BY-SA 4.0 · [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:20211010_073054_Clock_Square.jpg)\n\n09\n\n### JASIA\n\nAsian cooking under Ottoman stone arches\n\nJASIA was opened by two childhood friends from Jaffa, and its own account of itself is a deliberate collision: Japanese, Thai, Indian and Chinese cooking inside a historic stone building on Louis Pasteur Street that belongs to the Greek Orthodox Church, with stone arches, old wooden beams and fishing rope worked into the room. Sushi and wok dishes are the spine of the menu, and there is a bar attached. It is the outlier on this page and worth knowing about for exactly that reason, since almost everything else in Old Jaffa is Levantine, Greek or fish. The building is a good part of the reason to go.\n\nReservations through OnTopo from its own site.\n\n81% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun | Closed |\n| Mon-Sat | 11:30-23:30 |\n\n10\n\n### DeliAlhambra\n\nThe Jerusalem Boulevard breakfast locals use\n\nThis is the pick furthest from the tourist ground and the one most likely to have Jaffa residents at the next table. It is a delicatessen, coffee house and wine shop on Jerusalem Boulevard, the wide flat street that runs inland from the clock tower and carries the ordinary town rather than the restored one. It is listed as a breakfast room, a cheese shop, a coffee shop and a deli at once, which is accurate: the food is assembled from what is on the shelves, and in the evening the same room works as a wine bar. It stands on the same boulevard as the Alhambra, the art deco cinema of 1,000 seats that opened in May 1937 and where Umm Kulthum and Farid al-Atrash performed.\n\n95% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun-Tue | 07:30-18:00 |\n| Wed-Thu | 07:30-23:00 |\n| Fri | 08:00-16:00 |\n| Sat | Closed |\n\n![A narrow Jaffa market lane lined with stalls of framed pictures, second hand furniture and clothing racks, with shoppers walking between them](/_next/image?url=%2Fsite-photos%2Fsite-jaffa-jaffa-flea-market.webp&w=3840&q=75)\n\n[Rakoon](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:20171222_095154_December_2017_in_Tel_Aviv-Yafo.jpg \"Rakoon\") / CC0 · [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:20171222_095154_December_2017_in_Tel_Aviv-Yafo.jpg)\n\n11\n\n### Fish & Chips\n\nThe cheap lunch everyone walks past\n\nA plain fry counter at Jaffa Port, with prices that have nothing to do with the view a hundred metres up the hill. There is no dining room to speak of, no booking and not much menu: fish, calamari, chips and a chair on the quay. It is the right answer to a hungry hour between the harbour and the old town, and the one place in this guide where a full lunch costs less than a cocktail in the flea market. It is a small operation that keeps its own hours, so check the day before making a plan of it.\n\n86% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun | Closed |\n| Mon-Sat | 10:30-22:00 |\n\n12\n\n### Yaffa Knafeh\n\nHot knafeh with ice cream in it\n\nYaffa Knafeh was set up in 2018 by two friends, Farah Abu Nijam and Shahada Abu Shahada, who started by selling portions through a local grocery before opening their own place on Olei Zion Street. The house version puts ice cream into hot knafeh, which is what the shop is known for, and baklava, malabi and Turkish sweets sit alongside it with Arabic coffee and cardamom or mint tea. It is cheap, it is late, and it is the obvious end to an evening that started with fish. A second branch opened on Beit Eshel Street in April 2020.\n\n90% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\n#### Hours as listed\n\nOpening hours as published on Google Maps\n\n|  |  |\n| --- | --- |\n| Sun-Wed | 10:00-00:30 |\n| Thu-Fri | 10:00-01:00 |\n| Sat | 10:00-00:30 |\n\n![Fishing boats and tall masts moored along the quay at Jaffa harbour, with small orange dinghies on the water and the stone breakwater running out to the sea beyond](/_next/image?url=%2Fsite-photos%2Fsite-jaffa-jaffa-port.webp&w=3840&q=75)\n\n[שלמה רודד](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:109685_jaffa_sea_and_jaffa_PikiWiki_Israel.jpg \"שלמה רודד\") / CC BY 2.5 · [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:109685_jaffa_sea_and_jaffa_PikiWiki_Israel.jpg)\n\n13\n\n### The Jaffa Hotel, Tel Aviv\n\nThe only kosher dining room here\n\nNothing else on this page publishes a kashrut certificate, so a reader who keeps kosher starts here. Fattal's own page for the hotel describes it as a kosher hotel and its restaurant, Golda's, as a kosher dairy kitchen cooking Italian food with Jaffa ingredients; a second room, Giardino, is listed as coming. The setting is a 19th-century neo-Renaissance building reworked by the British architect John Pawson with Rami Gil, who kept the historic fabric and dropped modern rooms inside it, and the dining room is priced accordingly. A certified kitchen handles Friday evening and Saturday differently from the rest of the neighbourhood, with booking and payment taken beforehand, so ask the hotel how it runs Shabbat rather than assuming either way.\n\nBook the restaurant through the hotel. Confirm the Friday and Saturday arrangement when you do, because it will not be the arrangement anywhere else in this guide.\n\n80% of raters gave four or five stars.\n\nAs listed on Google Maps on 13 August 2026.\n\nThe food\n\n## Hummus before noon, mezze all afternoon, fish at the price of the view\n\nThe cheapest and oldest layer of Jaffa food is the hummus counter, and it is a morning trade rather than a lunch one. The reference address is Abu Hassan on Ha-Dolphin Street, which Asif, the Israeli food culture institute, dates to a cart Ali Karavan pushed from 1959 and a shop he opened in 1972. It sells hummus, ful and msabbaha, and the house dish is the triangle, which is all three in one plate. It opens at eight in the morning and shuts when the pots are empty, usually in the early afternoon, which is why it does not appear in the picks below and why anyone who wants it should treat it as breakfast. Two larger branches on Shivtei Israel Street keep longer hours and a longer menu.\n\nThe next layer up is the mezze table, and it is the thing most visitors get wrong. Sit down in a Levantine house here and a dozen or more small plates arrive unasked: pickles, beetroot, tahini, aubergine, hummus, hot pitta from a tabun oven. In most of these kitchens that spread is part of what the main course costs, not an extra. Two people who each order a starter and a main will leave most of it. The correct move is to order one grilled or fried thing between two, let the table fill, and add another dish afterwards if it is still needed.\n\nThe fish is the reason the restaurants are where they are. Jaffa harbour still lands boats, and the eating splits sharply by altitude: fry counters and plastic chairs at the quay, comfortable rooms with mezze and grilled fish on Louis Pasteur Street a short climb above, and the tables with a sea view on Kedumim Square, where a good part of the bill is the window. All three are legitimate. Deciding which one the meal is for, before walking up the hill, saves a lot of money or a lot of disappointment.\n\nThen there is the flea market, Shuk HaPishpeshim, which changed use rather than closed. The lanes around Nakhman, Rabbi Hanina and Rabbi Yohanan streets trade in second-hand furniture and rugs by day and in food, drink and live music after dark, and several of the rooms are furnished from the stalls immediately outside them. Inland from all of it, Jerusalem Boulevard is where Jaffa eats when nobody is watching: a wide street with the ordinary town along it, and, part way down, the Alhambra, the art deco cinema of 1,000 seats that Elias Al-Mor built in 1937 and that Umm Kulthum and Farid al-Atrash sang in.\n\n![A spread of small mezze dishes seen from above](https://images.unsplash.com/photo-1653611540493-b3a896319fbf?ixid=M3wxMDE5Mzc0fDB8MXxzZWFyY2h8MzB8fElzcmFlbGklMjBmb29kJTIwaHVtbXVzJTIwZmFsYWZlbCUyMG1lenplfGVufDF8MHx8fDE3ODYwMjE2MzF8MA&ixlib=rb-4.1.0&w=1600&h=727&fit=crop&crop=entropy&auto=format&q=75)\n\n[Alexandra Tran](https://unsplash.com/@alexgoesglobal?utm_source=israel_com&utm_medium=referral) / [Unsplash](https://unsplash.com/photos/a-wooden-table-topped-with-bowls-of-food-IjrBf8rtKXE?utm_source=israel_com&utm_medium=referral)\n\nKashrut\n\n## Saturday is a normal trading day, and Monday often is not\n\nIsrael has no Shabbat law. The Israel Democracy Institute's guide to the status quo sets out what exists instead: the 1951 Hours of Work and Rest Law, which guarantees a weekly rest of at least 36 consecutive hours falling on Saturday for Jewish workers, and the Municipalities Ordinance, which lets each local authority regulate the opening and closing of shops, restaurants, cafes, bars, cinemas and theatres with the claims of religious tradition in mind. Tel Aviv-Yafo is at the permissive end of that range, and Jaffa sits inside Tel Aviv-Yafo. It is also a mixed city whose Arab residents are not covered by the rest-day provisions at all. The combined result is that Saturday here is an ordinary trading day and Friday evening is a busy one.\n\nThe published weeks of the picks below bear that out and add a twist worth knowing. Kalamata lists Friday and Saturday 11:00 to 23:00. Raisa publishes Saturday from ten in the morning with a Saturday brunch. Faruk BaShuk puts live music on at four o'clock on a Friday afternoon, which is the hour the market itself is winding down. Kabakeh is the sharpest case: its booking page opens it Wednesday to Saturday and closes it Sunday, Monday and Tuesday. A visitor who plans Jaffa around avoiding Shabbat will find the restaurants open and may find them shut on a Monday instead.\n\nWhat does stop on Friday afternoon is the retail. The flea market stalls wind down and public transport ends across the country until Saturday evening, Israel Railways and the Tel Aviv light rail included. None of that closes the restaurants. It does mean Saturday in Jaffa is best planned as a walking and eating day rather than a shopping one, which is roughly what the neighbourhood is for anyway.\n\nFor anyone who keeps kosher this cuts hard the other way, and it is worth being exact rather than reassuring. Of the thirteen picks here, one publishes a kashrut statement: Fattal's page for The Jaffa Hotel describes it as a kosher hotel and its restaurant Golda's as a kosher dairy kitchen. Two of the others put the matter beyond doubt in the opposite direction, since Captain Hook serves oysters and The Old Man and the Sea puts prawns and calamari on its own menu. The remaining ten say nothing, which is not the same as being uncertified but should be read as an answer. One legal point has changed recently: on 15 July 2026 the Knesset passed a law by 46 votes to 41 restoring the Chief Rabbinate's exclusive right to issue kosher certification, reversing the reform that had let private Orthodox bodies certify in their own name. A certificate is a dated paper document displayed on the premises, naming the body that issued it, and it expires. If kashrut matters, read the door rather than a listing, an aggregator or this page.\n\n![The arched front of the Ilana Goor Museum, its name across the wall](/site-photos/site-jaffa-ilana-goor-museum-3.webp)\n\n[Laliv Gal](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:PikiWiki_Israel_45748_Geography_of_Israel.jpg \"Laliv Gal\") / CC BY 2.5 · [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:PikiWiki_Israel_45748_Geography_of_Israel.jpg)\n\nPracticalities\n\n## Booking, tipping, tap water and the salads nobody ordered\n\nBooking runs through OnTopo, the Israeli platform, rather than by email. Kabakeh, Kalamata, Raisa, JASIA and Faruk BaShuk all send reservations there from their own pages; several also take a WhatsApp message or a telephone call, and the harbour counters take neither because they have no tables to hold. Friday and Saturday evenings and the Saturday brunch services are the ones that need booking in advance. The rooms in the flea market are small, which means they fill before the large ones on the square do, not after.\n\nTipping is not optional and it is not on the bill. Israeli restaurants do not normally add a service charge, and the customary tip is around ten to fifteen per cent left on top. Since 1 January 2019 that money has been salary rather than a gift: the National Labour Court ruled in March 2018 that tips count as wages whether they pass through the till or straight into a waiter's hand, carrying income tax, pension and national insurance. Practically, ask whether the tip can go on the card, because tills vary and some places still want it in cash. Cash is worth carrying in Jaffa anyway, because the market stalls often do not take anything else.\n\nThe printed price is the whole price. Israeli VAT has stood at 18 per cent since 2025 and is already inside the menu figure, so the only arithmetic left at the end of a meal is the tip. Tap water is safe and free everywhere, and asking for it by name is normal; bottled water will otherwise arrive and be charged for.\n\nOne thing about quantity. Neither the Levantine houses nor the fish restaurants reward ordering a course each: the mezze is included, the portions are built for the middle of the table, and both work out cheaper than they look once that is understood.\n\n![The Jaffa Clock Tower standing in its square, strings of flags running from it](/site-photos/site-jaffa-jaffa-clock-tower-2.webp)\n\n[Fallaner](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Holy_Land_2022_(1)_P043_Jaffa_Clock_square.jpg \"Fallaner\") / CC BY-SA 4.0 · [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Holy_Land_2022_(1)_P043_Jaffa_Clock_square.jpg)\n\nGeography\n\n## The square, the harbour, the market and the boulevard\n\nKedumim Square is the top of the hill and the top of the price range: the view, the restored stone, the tour groups, and the two rooms on this page that earn the setting rather than merely occupying it. Immediately below it, Louis Pasteur Street is a short lane carrying several of Jaffa's better known Arab kitchens one beside the next, along with a couple of cafes. It is the most concentrated eating address in the neighbourhood and it is thirty seconds off the square.\n\nThe harbour is the cheap ground: a working fishing port with a fleet, a few sheds, a row of counters and a quay to eat on. It is also the right place to be at the end of the afternoon, when the boats come in and the light goes off the water onto the stone.\n\nThe flea market is the evening. Nakhman, Rabbi Hanina and Rabbi Yohanan streets hold most of the bars, bistros and cafes that grew into the retail units as the second-hand traders thinned out, and the lanes stay busy long after the tarpaulins come down. It is the one part of Jaffa that is better after dark than before, and it is where the live music is.\n\nJerusalem Boulevard is where a Jaffa resident eats: flat, inland from the clock tower, restored for nobody's benefit, and priced for people who live there. Anyone with a spare hour and no appetite for another sea view should walk it.\n\n![Kedumim Square in Old Jaffa with St Peter's Church and its bell tower at the far end](/site-photos/site-jaffa-kedumim-square-2.webp)\n\n[Beko](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:St._Peters_church,_Tel_Aviv_Yaffo_(3).jpg \"Beko\") / CC BY-SA 4.0 · [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:St._Peters_church,_Tel_Aviv_Yaffo_(3).jpg)\n\nThe full list\n\n## Every place to eat we found in Jaffa\n\nAll 36 of them, ranked by rating weighted for Sources and further reading\n\nEvery link below was opened and checked when this page was written. Official statements are marked as such: they are the subject's own account, not an independent one.\n\nThe restaurant's own booking page: its relationship to Ezba, its Kedumim Square address and the Wednesday to Saturday week.\n\nThe Kikar Kdumim address, the name taken from the Greek town and its olives, the two-level room and the Friday and Saturday hours.\n\nThe 1999 founding, the daily salads and tabun pitta, and the fried prawns and calamari on its own menu.\n\nThe owner's statement about the room, the fish patties and lamb cigars, the seven-day week and the Saturday brunch hours.\n\nThe Nakhman Street address, the happy hour, the Tuesday evening and Friday afternoon live music and the Friday brunch.\n\nThe two founders from Jaffa, the Japanese, Thai, Indian and Chinese menu, and the Greek Orthodox stone building on Louis Pasteur Street.\n\nThe 2018 founding by Farah Abu Nijam and Shahada Abu Shahada, the knafeh with ice cream, and the second branch on Beit Eshel Street.\n\nThe hotel's own statement that it is kosher and that Golda's is a kosher dairy restaurant, plus the 19th-century building and its architects.\n\nThe 15 July 2026 vote and the reform it reversed, which is what a kashrut certificate now means and who may issue one.\n\nThe National Labour Court ruling of March 2018 and the 1 January 2019 date from which tips counted as salary.\n\nThe 1959 cart, the 1972 shop on Ha-Dolphin Street, the triangle of hummus, ful and msabbaha, the morning hours and the two later branches.\n\nThe 1951 Hours of Work and Rest Law, the 36-hour weekly rest, and the Municipalities Ordinance power over restaurant and cafe opening.\n\nThe Israeli VAT rate of 18 per cent, which is already inside every menu price.\n\nThe 1,000-seat art deco cinema on Jerusalem Boulevard, opened May 1937 to a design by Elias Al-Mor, and the performers it drew.\n\n## Where to eat in Jaffa: common questions\n\nVery little, and only one entry in this guide states it. Fattal's page for The Jaffa Hotel describes the hotel as kosher and its restaurant Golda's as a kosher dairy kitchen, which makes the hotel dining rooms the realistic answer for a visitor who needs certification. Jaffa's restaurant trade is largely Arab-owned or seafood-based, neither of which points towards a certificate, and most places publish nothing on the subject. If it matters, read the dated certificate on the door, or eat in central Tel Aviv where the seafront hotels carry certified kitchens.\n\nMost of them do, and Friday evening is one of the busiest services of the week. Israel has no national Shabbat law: the Municipalities Ordinance leaves opening to the local authority, and Tel Aviv-Yafo is permissive. What stops on Friday afternoon is the retail and the transport, the flea market stalls, the trains and the light rail, not the kitchens.\n\nThe shopping, not the eating. The flea market's second-hand stalls are the main casualty, along with public transport across the country from Friday afternoon to Saturday evening. Restaurants, bars and cafes trade through it, and several flea market rooms publish Saturday hours specifically, so a Saturday in Jaffa works well as a walking and eating day.\n\nAway from the harbour and the square, mostly. Jerusalem Boulevard and the streets running off it are flat, unrestored and priced for residents, which is where DeliAlhambra sits, and the hummus counters on and around Ha-Dolphin and Yefet Streets are a morning habit rather than a tourist stop. The flea market is genuinely mixed: locals and visitors in the same rooms, especially on a weekday evening.\n\nYes, if you pick the right altitude. The counters on the quay at Jaffa Port fry what the boats land and cost very little; the restaurants a short climb above charge considerably more for a comfortable room, and the tables on Kedumim Square charge more again for the window. All three sell fish from the same sea. What differs is the seat.\n\nFor Friday and Saturday evenings, yes, and for Saturday brunch. Nearly everything here takes bookings through OnTopo from its own website, and several places also answer a WhatsApp message. The small rooms in the flea market fill earliest; the harbour counters have nothing to book.\n\nThe name that comes up more than any other is Abu Hassan on Ha-Dolphin Street, which Asif dates to a cart from 1959 and a shop from 1972, with two larger branches on Shivtei Israel Street. It sells hummus, ful and msabbaha and the house triangle of all three, opens at eight in the morning and closes when the food runs out, usually in the early afternoon. Treat it as breakfast, not lunch. It is not in the picks above because it is not among the places listed on this page.\n\nUnusually well, because the default opening of a Levantine meal is a table of vegetable dishes. Hummus, ful, msabbaha, tahini, aubergine, pickles, beetroot and hot bread will feed a vegetarian properly in almost every restaurant here without a single special request. Vegans should ask about labneh and yoghurt, which turn up in more dishes than the menu suggests.\n\nThe range within one square kilometre is very wide, which is why this guide covers both ends of it. Whatever the bill, the printed price is the whole price: VAT has been 18 per cent since 2025 and is already inside the menu figure, and no service charge is normally added. Budget ten to fifteen per cent on top for the tip and carry some cash, because the market stalls often take nothing else.\n\nBoth, but they are different neighbourhoods. Mornings and lunchtimes belong to the hummus counters, the flea market cafes and the harbour fry shops, several of which shut in the early afternoon. Evenings belong to the square, the fish restaurants and the flea market bars, which is when the live music starts and when the lanes are at their best.\n\n## Keep exploring\n\nTools, trackers and guides for Israel and the Jewish world: live data, key dates, and practical help for visiting, moving and investing.\n\n## News and Israel.com\n\n## Calculators\n\n## Calendar and data\n\n## Hebrew and aliyah\n\n## Places and business\n\n© 2026 Israel.com LLC.
